Boundless generator

Generator ceiling: up to 1,000,000,000 scales per file, generated on demand by the file AI — not stored files. Every generated record is labeled src "generated". Signature Time-Series Engine: A Design for Forecasting from History JAH-PREDS-000001 · 1

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Time-Series Engine is a Signature-made design for forecasting a series from its own history, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes a series, differences it until it is steady, fits an autoregressive and moving-average model, checks that the residuals look like noise, and only then forecasts. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of ARIMA from Box and Jenkins in 1970. The design takes the identify-estimate-check-forecast discipline the public record describes, and makes the check a gate: the engine refuses to forecast when the residuals are not white noise, and says so. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Time-Series Engine is a Signature-made design for forecasting a series from its own history, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes a series, differences it until it is steady, fits an autoregressive and moving-average model, checks that the residuals look like noise, and only then forecasts. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Time-Series Engine, a Signature-made design-study for forecasting from history, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the series as a sequence of dated values. Step 2: difference it until it is steady. Step 3: fit the model, and check the residuals. Step 4: forecast only when the check passes, and report the forecast with its interval. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step with illustrative design values: it takes five forecast values, 110, 125, 118, 140, and 132, against reference values of 112, 123, 120, 138, and 134, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_design": 2.0, "rmse_design": 2.0, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the engine differences, fits, checks, and forecasts only when the check passes, and the worked example shows the error arithmetic. The gaps are 2, 2, 2, 2, and 2, giving a mean absolute error of 2.0 and a root mean square error of 2.0, with a largest gap of 2. The result that matters is the design judgment: an engine that refuses to forecast on a failed check is honest, and one that forecasts anyway is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 2.0, max_err: 2, n: 5, rmse: 2.0.

When It Applies

The Signature Time-Series Engine design applies as a study for anyone building a forecaster: it shows how to difference, fit, check, and gate, and gives the arithmetic for checking the work. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the structure breaks, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The engine is a design, not a system, so it has never forecast a real series, and its values are illustrative. The design assumes the structure is stable, which real series often are not, and it cannot foresee a break that has no precedent in the data. The design is a study of how a forecasting engine should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such an engine exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 2, 2, 2, 2, and 2, which sum to 10, so the mean absolute error is 10 divided by 5, which is 2.0. The squared gaps are 4, 4, 4, 4, and 4, which sum to 20, so the mean squared error is 4.0 and the root mean square error is the square root of 4.0, which is 2.0. The largest single gap is 2. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of dependence over time: a value today depends on the values before it, and that dependence can be measured and used. The design follows the public evidence that ARIMA works when the dependence is stable and fails when the structure changes, and it answers by making the residual check a gate. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(110, 112), (125, 123), (118, 120), (140, 138), (132, 134)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPREDS000001:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(110, 112), (125, 123), (118, 120), (140, 138), (132, 134)]
        def compute():
            errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
            mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
            rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
            max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
            n = len(pairs)
            out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPREDS000001().run())

Signature Regression Engine: A Design for Transparent Prediction JAH-PREDS-000002 · 2

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Regression Engine is a Signature-made design for linear prediction that can be read, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design fits a line by least squares, shows the line, shows the residuals, and refuses to predict when the residuals show a pattern the line has missed. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of least squares from 1805 and Galton's regression from 1886. The design takes the method's honesty, that the residuals show what the line missed, and makes the residual check a gate: the engine reports the pattern it finds, and will not present a prediction from a line whose residuals are patterned. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Regression Engine is a Signature-made design for linear prediction that can be read, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design fits a line by least squares, shows the line, shows the residuals, and refuses to predict when the residuals show a pattern the line has missed. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Regression Engine, a Signature-made design-study for transparent linear prediction, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the data as pairs of an input and an outcome. Step 2: fit the least-squares line. Step 3: examine the residuals for patterns. Step 4: predict only when the residuals look like noise, and report the residual spread as the uncertainty. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step with illustrative design values: it takes five predicted outcomes, 12, 17, 22, 27, and 32, against actual outcomes of 13, 16, 23, 26, and 33, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_design": 1.0, "rmse_design": 1.0, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The gaps are 1, 1, 1, 1, and 1, giving a mean absolute error of 1.0 and a root mean square error of 1.0, with a largest gap of 1. Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of linear association: when two quantities move together, a line can summarize how much one moves when the other moves, and the residuals test whether the summary is honest. The design follows the public evidence that regression is transparent and that its residuals announce its failures, and it makes the announcement a gate. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(12, 13), (17, 16), (22, 23), (27, 26), (32, 33)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Classifier Studio: A Design for Auditable Classification JAH-PREDS-000003 · 3

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Classifier Studio is a Signature-made design for classification that can be audited, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design fits a logistic model, reports every coefficient as an odds ratio, calibrates the probabilities, and shows the threshold and the costs of each kind of error. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of logistic regression from Berkson in 1944. The design takes the method's interpretability, that each coefficient can be read, and adds the audit: the calibration is checked, the threshold is stated, and the error rates are reported by group. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Classifier Studio is a Signature-made design for classification that can be audited, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design fits a logistic model, reports every coefficient as an odds ratio, calibrates the probabilities, and shows the threshold and the costs of each kind of error. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Classifier Studio, a Signature-made design-study for auditable classification, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the data as inputs with a binary outcome. Step 2: fit the logistic model, and report each coefficient as an odds ratio. Step 3: calibrate the probabilities, and check them against the observed frequencies. Step 4: state the threshold and the costs of each kind of error, and report the error rates by group. The demo runs a small worked example of the calibration step with illustrative design values: it takes five predicted probabilities, 0.75, 0.35, 0.85, 0.55, and 0.45, against outcomes of 1, 0, 1, 1, and 0, and reports the Brier score and the mean predicted probability. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"brier_design": 0.1225, "mean_p_design": 0.59, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the studio fits, calibrates, states the threshold, and reports by group, and the worked example shows the calibration arithmetic. The squared gaps are 0.0625, 0.1225, 0.0225, 0.2025, and 0.2025, which sum to 0.6125, so the Brier score is 0.1225, and the mean predicted probability is 0.59, with 5 hits out of 5. The result that matters is the design judgment: a classifier that states its threshold and reports by group can be audited, and one that does not cannot, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: brier: 0.1225, hits: 5, mean_p: 0.59, n: 5.

When It Applies

The Signature Classifier Studio design applies as a study for anyone building a classifier that must be audited: it shows how to fit, calibrate, and report by group. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the boundary is curved, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The studio is a design, not a system, so it has never classified a real case, and its probabilities are illustrative. The design assumes the log-odds are linear, which is not always true, and it cannot capture a curved boundary. The design is a study of how an auditable classifier should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a studio exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the Brier score. The five predictions and outcomes give squared gaps of 0.0625, 0.1225, 0.0225, 0.2025, and 0.2025, which sum to 0.6125, and 0.6125 divided by 5 is 0.1225. The mean predicted probability is 2.95 divided by 5, which is 0.59, and 5 of the 5 predictions were hits. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: Brier = (1/n) * sum (p_i - o_i)^2, where p_i is the predicted probability and o_i is 1 when the event happened and 0 when it did not. The equation states the calibration check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted probabilities and outcomes, squares each gap, and averages them for the Brier score.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of modeling odds: the log-odds are modeled as a linear function of the inputs, which keeps the probabilities bounded and the coefficients interpretable. The design follows the public evidence that logistic regression survives because it can be read, and it adds the fairness audit, because a classifier that is accurate on average can still be unfair in its errors. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A predicted probability is calibrated when events it calls likely actually happen often; the Brier score measures that gap, with 0 for perfect prediction.. Working demo program

import hashlib
probs = [(0.75, 1), (0.35, 0), (0.85, 1), (0.55, 1), (0.45, 0)]
def compute():
    brier = round(sum((p - o) ** 2 for p, o in probs) / len(probs), 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in probs) / len(probs), 4)
    hits = sum(1 for p, o in probs if (p >= 0.5) == bool(o))
    n = len(probs)
    out = {"brier": brier, "mean_p": mean_p, "hits": hits, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Maintenance Predictor: A Design for Listening to Machines JAH-PREDS-000004 · 4

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Maintenance Predictor is a Signature-made design for predicting machine failure from sensor data, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design watches vibration, temperature, and sound, establishes the normal pattern for each, and predicts the remaining useful life from the drift, reporting the prediction with its uncertainty and the sensor that drove it. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of predictive maintenance from vibration analysis in the mid-twentieth century through the sensor-rich monitoring of the 2010s. The design takes the practice's core, that degradation shows in the data before the failure does, and adds the attribution: every prediction names the sensor that drove it, so the engineer can check the reading rather than merely trust the number. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Maintenance Predictor is a Signature-made design for predicting machine failure from sensor data, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design watches vibration, temperature, and sound, establishes the normal pattern for each, and predicts the remaining useful life from the drift, reporting the prediction with its uncertainty and the sensor that drove it. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Maintenance Predictor, a Signature-made design-study for predicting machine failure, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the sensor readings as a time series. Step 2: establish the normal pattern for each reading, and watch for drift away from it. Step 3: predict the remaining useful life from the drift, using the history of similar machines. Step 4: report the prediction with its uncertainty and the sensor that drove it. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step with illustrative design values: it takes five predicted remaining-life values, 100, 80, 130, 70, and 95 days, against actual values of 98, 83, 128, 74, and 93 days, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_design": 2.6, "rmse_design": 2.7203, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the predictor watches, predicts, and attributes, and the worked example shows the error arithmetic. The gaps are 2, 3, 2, 4, and 2, giving a mean absolute error of 2.6 and a root mean square error of 2.7203, with a largest gap of 4. The result that matters is the design judgment: a prediction that names its sensor can be checked, and one that does not cannot, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 2.6, max_err: 4, n: 5, rmse: 2.7203.

When It Applies

The Signature Maintenance Predictor design applies as a study for anyone building a maintenance system: it shows how to watch, predict, and attribute, and gives the arithmetic for checking the work. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where failures are sudden, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The predictor is a design, not a system, so it has never predicted a real failure, and its values are illustrative. The design assumes the degradation is gradual and the history is rich, which is not always true, and a failure mode never seen before is invisible to it. The design is a study of how a maintenance predictor should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a predictor exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 2, 3, 2, 4, and 2, which sum to 13, so the mean absolute error is 13 divided by 5, which is 2.6. The squared gaps are 4, 9, 4, 16, and 4, which sum to 37, so the mean squared error is 7.4 and the root mean square error is the square root of 7.4, which is 2.7203. The largest single gap is 4. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of degradation: machines wear in patterns, and the pattern shows in the sensor data before the failure does. The design follows the public evidence that predictive maintenance works where the degradation is gradual and the history is rich, and it adds the attribution, because a prediction without a reason is a number without a check. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(100, 98), (80, 83), (130, 128), (70, 74), (95, 93)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()

        print(f"Result: {result}")

Signature Weather Engine: A Design for Ensemble Forecasting JAH-PREDS-000005 · 5

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Weather Engine is a Signature-made design for ensemble weather forecasting, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the present state of the atmosphere, runs the model many times with slightly different starting states, and reports the forecast as a distribution, with the spread showing the uncertainty. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of numerical weather prediction from Richardson in 1922 through the first ENIAC forecast in 1950. The design takes the ensemble idea, that the spread of the runs measures the uncertainty, and makes the spread part of the forecast rather than a footnote. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Weather Engine is a Signature-made design for ensemble weather forecasting, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the present state of the atmosphere, runs the model many times with slightly different starting states, and reports the forecast as a distribution, with the spread showing the uncertainty. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Weather Engine, a Signature-made design-study for ensemble weather forecasting, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the present state from observations. Step 2: run the model many times with slightly different starting states. Step 3: collect the runs into a distribution, and read the forecast and its spread. Step 4: report the forecast with its spread, so the user knows how confident to be. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step with illustrative design values: it takes five forecast temperatures, 20, 23, 17, 26, and 21 degrees, against observed temperatures of 19, 24, 18, 25, and 22 degrees, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_design": 1.0, "rmse_design": 1.0, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the engine runs the ensemble, reads the distribution, and reports the spread, and the worked example shows the error arithmetic. The gaps are 1, 1, 1, 1, and 1, giving a mean absolute error of 1.0 and a root mean square error of 1.0, with a largest gap of 1. The result that matters is the design judgment: a forecast that reports its spread is honest, and one that reports a single number is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 1.0, max_err: 1, n: 5, rmse: 1.0.

When It Applies

The Signature Weather Engine design applies as a study for anyone building an ensemble forecaster: it shows how to run, collect, and report the spread. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the observations are thin, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The engine is a design, not a system, so it has never forecast real weather, and its values are illustrative. The design assumes the physics is known and the observations are dense, which is not always true, and it cannot foresee a storm that has not yet formed. The design is a study of how an ensemble forecaster should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such an engine exists or works.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of the atmosphere as a physical system with chaotic limits: the future follows from the present by calculation, but small differences grow with time, so the skill fades with lead time and the spread measures that fading. The design follows the public evidence that ensemble forecasting is the standard, and it makes the spread the forecast's headline rather than its footnote. pairs = [(20, 19), (23, 24), (17, 18), (26, 25), (21, 22)]
def compute():

    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)

The Signature Finance Forecaster is a Signature-made design for forecasting risk rather than price, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the efficient-market lesson, that price is hard to predict because it already reflects what is known, and answers by forecasting what can be forecast: the volatility, the correlation, and the range of outcomes. History

The design takes the lesson of that history, that risk models failed in the crisis because they assumed normal times, and adds the stress test: every forecast is accompanied by a worst case, and the worst case is reported alongside the central one. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.4, 0.3), (-0.2, -0.4), (0.7, 0.8), (0.1, 0.0), (-0.3, -0.2)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Epidemic Forecaster: A Design for Ranges, Not Single Numbers JAH-PREDS-000007 · 7

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Epidemic Forecaster is a Signature-made design for forecasting an outbreak as a range rather than a single number, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the SIR frame, runs it under several assumptions about the reproduction number and the interventions, and reports the range of outcomes with the assumption that drives each end. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of SIR models from Kermack and McKendrick in 1927 and the COVID-19 forecasts of 2020. The design takes the lesson of 2020, that the forecasts varied widely because the numbers were not yet known, and makes the range the forecast: a single number without its range is not reported at all. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Epidemic Forecaster is a Signature-made design for forecasting an outbreak as a range rather than a single number, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the SIR frame, runs it under several assumptions about the reproduction number and the interventions, and reports the range of outcomes with the assumption that drives each end. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Epidemic Forecaster, a Signature-made design-study for forecasting outbreaks as ranges, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the early case counts and estimate the reproduction number with its uncertainty. Step 2: run the SIR model under several assumptions, and collect the outcomes into a range. Step 3: name the assumption that drives each end of the range. Step 4: report the range, the assumptions, and the update schedule, and update as data arrives. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step with illustrative design values: it takes five predicted weekly case counts, 900, 1400, 1900, 1700, and 1200, against actual counts of 880, 1420, 1880, 1720, and 1180, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_design": 20.0, "rmse_design": 20.0, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the forecaster runs the range, names the drivers, and updates, and the worked example shows the error arithmetic. The gaps are 20, 20, 20, 20, and 20, giving a mean absolute error of 20.0 and a root mean square error of 20.0, with a largest gap of 20. The result that matters is the design judgment: a forecast that reports its range and its drivers can be used, and one that reports a single number cannot, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 20.0, max_err: 20, n: 5, rmse: 20.0.

When It Applies

The Signature Epidemic Forecaster design applies as a study for anyone building an outbreak forecaster: it shows how to run the range, name the drivers, and update. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply as public health advice, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The forecaster is a design, not a system, so it has never forecast a real outbreak, and its values are illustrative. The design assumes the population mixes evenly, which real populations do not, and it cannot foresee a change in behaviour that the forecast itself causes. The design is a study of how an epidemic forecaster should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a forecaster exists or works, or for public health advice of any kind.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 20, 20, 20, 20, and 20, which sum to 100, so the mean absolute error is 100 divided by 5, which is 20.0. The squared gaps are 400, 400, 400, 400, and 400, which sum to 2000, so the mean squared error is 400.0 and the root mean square error is the square root of 400.0, which is 20.0. The largest single gap is 20. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of transmission: an outbreak grows when each case infects more than one other, and the reproduction number is the whole story in a single number. The design follows the public evidence that the number is estimated rather than measured, and that a small error in it grows into a large error in the peak, and it answers by reporting the range the uncertainty creates. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. import hashlib
pairs = [(900, 880), (1400, 1420), (1900, 1880), (1700, 1720), (1200, 1180)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

The Signature Demand Forecaster design applies as a study for anyone building a demand forecaster: it shows how to track three components and flag breaks. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 4, 4, 4, 4, and 4, which sum to 20, so the mean absolute error is 20 divided by 5, which is 4.0. The squared gaps are 16, 16, 16, 16, and 16, which sum to 80, so the mean squared error is 16.0 and the root mean square error is the square root of 16.0, which is 4.0. The largest single gap is 4. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of stable patterns: demand has a level, a trend, and a season, and those three change slowly enough to be estimated from history. The design follows the public evidence that Holt-Winters is still the baseline because it is simple and hard to beat on stable series, and it adds the break flag because the evidence also shows that a break defeats it. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(380, 376), (440, 444), (360, 364), (470, 466), (410, 414)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Churn Predictor: A Design for Finding the Leavers Early JAH-PREDS-000009 · 9

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Churn Predictor is a Signature-made design for predicting which customers will leave, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes each customer's usage, payments, and support contacts, turns them into a probability of leaving, and ranks the customers so that the retention action goes to the top of the list. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of churn modeling from telecom in the 1990s. The design takes the practice's core, that falling usage and late payments are the visible part of a decision to leave, and adds the action rule: the retention offer goes to the customers whose decision it can change, not to those who would stay anyway. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Churn Predictor is a Signature-made design for predicting which customers will leave, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes each customer's usage, payments, and support contacts, turns them into a probability of leaving, and ranks the customers so that the retention action goes to the top of the list. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Churn Predictor, a Signature-made design-study for predicting customer churn, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take each customer's history as features. Step 2: fit a classifier that turns the features into a probability of leaving. Step 3: rank the customers by probability, and flag the top of the list. Step 4: measure the outcome, the customers kept and the offers wasted, and update the model. The demo runs a small worked example of the calibration step with illustrative design values: it takes five predicted churn probabilities, 0.65, 0.25, 0.8, 0.4, and 0.55, against outcomes of 1, 0, 1, 0, and 1, and reports the Brier score and the mean predicted probability. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"brier_design": 0.1175, "mean_p_design": 0.53, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the predictor ranks, flags, and measures, and the worked example shows the calibration arithmetic. The squared gaps are 0.1225, 0.0625, 0.04, 0.16, and 0.2025, which sum to 0.5875, so the Brier score is 0.1175, and the mean predicted probability is 0.53, with 5 hits out of 5. The result that matters is the design judgment: a predictor that measures the customers kept is useful, and one that reports only its accuracy is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: brier: 0.1175, hits: 5, mean_p: 0.53, n: 5.

When It Applies

The Signature Churn Predictor design applies as a study for anyone building a churn system: it shows how to rank, flag, and measure the customers kept. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the reasons for leaving are invisible, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The predictor is a design, not a system, so it has never predicted a real churn, and its probabilities are illustrative. The design assumes the reasons for leaving are visible in the data, which they often are not, and it decays as the customer base changes. The design is a study of how a churn predictor should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a predictor exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the Brier score. The five predictions and outcomes give squared gaps of 0.1225, 0.0625, 0.04, 0.16, and 0.2025, which sum to 0.5875, and 0.5875 divided by 5 is 0.1175. The mean predicted probability is 2.65 divided by 5, which is 0.53, and 5 of the 5 predictions were hits. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: Brier = (1/n) * sum (p_i - o_i)^2, where p_i is the predicted probability and o_i is 1 when the event happened and 0 when it did not. The equation states the calibration check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted probabilities and outcomes, squares each gap, and averages them for the Brier score.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of behaviour as a signal: falling usage, late payment, and complaints are the visible part of a decision to leave, and the model reads them before the decision is final. The design follows the public evidence that churn models work where the behavioural data is rich, and it adds the action rule, because the value is in the customers kept rather than in the accuracy. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A predicted probability is calibrated when events it calls likely actually happen often; the Brier score measures that gap, with 0 for perfect prediction.. Working demo program

import hashlib
probs = [(0.65, 1), (0.25, 0), (0.8, 1), (0.4, 0), (0.55, 1)]
def compute():
    brier = round(sum((p - o) ** 2 for p, o in probs) / len(probs), 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in probs) / len(probs), 4)
    hits = sum(1 for p, o in probs if (p >= 0.5) == bool(o))
    n = len(probs)
    out = {"brier": brier, "mean_p": mean_p, "hits": hits, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Credit Forecaster is a Signature-made design for credit scoring that ranks applicants and audits the ranking, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the applicant's history, turns it into a score, and reports the score with the reasons, the error rates by group, and the override path, so that a decision can be explained, appealed, and checked for bias. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of credit scoring from Fair Isaac in 1956 and the FICO score in 1989. The design takes the score's strength, that it ranks consistently, and adds the audit the public record shows is necessary: the error rates by group, the reasons for an adverse decision, and a human who can override. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Credit Forecaster is a Signature-made design for credit scoring that ranks applicants and audits the ranking, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the applicant's history, turns it into a score, and reports the score with the reasons, the error rates by group, and the override path, so that a decision can be explained, appealed, and checked for bias. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Credit Forecaster, a Signature-made design-study for credit scoring with an audit, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the applicant's history as features. Step 2: fit the scoring model, and convert the probability into a score. Step 3: report the score with the reasons, the error rates by group, and the override path. Step 4: let a human review the adverse decisions, and audit the scores regularly. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step with illustrative design values: it takes five predicted scores, 700, 640, 760, 590, and 680, against reference scores of 696, 644, 756, 594, and 676, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_design": 4.0, "rmse_design": 4.0, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the forecaster scores, explains, audits, and allows override, and the worked example shows the error arithmetic. The gaps are 4, 4, 4, 4, and 4, giving a mean absolute error of 4.0 and a root mean square error of 4.0, with a largest gap of 4. The result that matters is the design judgment: a score that can be explained, appealed, and audited is a tool, and one that cannot is a verdict, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 4.0, max_err: 4, n: 5, rmse: 4.0.

When It Applies

The Signature Credit Forecaster design applies as a study for anyone building a credit system: it shows how to score, explain, audit, and override. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the file is thin, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The forecaster is a design, not a system, so it has never scored a real applicant, and its values are illustrative. The design assumes the history is rich and the score is audited, which is not always true, and it cannot see an applicant's circumstances. The design is a study of how a credit forecaster should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a forecaster exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 4, 4, 4, 4, and 4, which sum to 20, so the mean absolute error is 20 divided by 5, which is 4.0. The squared gaps are 16, 16, 16, 16, and 16, which sum to 80, so the mean squared error is 16.0 and the root mean square error is the square root of 16.0, which is 4.0. The largest single gap is 4. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of repayment as a predictable behaviour: payment history predicts future payment, and the model's job is to weigh the history correctly. The design follows the public evidence that a score must be audited for bias as carefully as for accuracy, because an accurate score that is unfair is still a failure. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(700, 696), (640, 644), (760, 756), (590, 594), (680, 676)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPREDS000010:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(700, 696), (640, 644), (760, 756), (590, 594), (680, 676)]
        def compute():
            errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
            mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
            rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
            max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
            n = len(pairs)
            out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPREDS000010().run())

Signature Metric Dashboard: A Design for Reporting Both Errors JAH-PREDS-000011 · 11

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Metric Dashboard is a Signature-made design for reporting forecast error honestly, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error together, with the bias and the largest error, so that the reader can see both the average and the unevenness. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of forecast evaluation from the twentieth century and the Makridakis competitions from 1982. The design takes the discipline the public record shows, that no single number tells the whole story, and makes it a dashboard: four numbers, always together, never alone. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Metric Dashboard is a Signature-made design for reporting forecast error honestly, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of honest measurement: an error metric should be in the units of the thing measured, it should treat over-forecasts and under-forecasts alike, and it should not hide a bias. import hashlib
pairs = [(4, 0), (2, 0), (5, 0), (3, 0), (6, 0)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPREDS000011:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(4, 0), (2, 0), (5, 0), (3, 0), (6, 0)]
        def compute():
            errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
            mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
            rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
            max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
            n = len(pairs)
            out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPREDS000011().run())

Signature Calibration Studio: A Design for Honest Probabilities JAH-PREDS-000012 · 12

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Calibration Studio is a Signature-made design for calibrating predicted probabilities, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes a model's probabilities, plots them against the observed frequencies in a reliability diagram, scores them with the Brier score, and recalibrates them when they are systematically overconfident or underconfident. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of calibration from Brier's 1950 paper. The design takes the reliability diagram and the Brier score the public record describes, and makes them a studio: the diagram is drawn, the score is computed, and the recalibration is applied, all in one place. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Calibration Studio is a Signature-made design for calibrating predicted probabilities, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes a model's probabilities, plots them against the observed frequencies in a reliability diagram, scores them with the Brier score, and recalibrates them when they are systematically overconfident or underconfident. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Calibration Studio, a Signature-made design-study for calibrating predicted probabilities, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the predicted probabilities and the outcomes as pairs. Step 2: group the predictions into bins, and compute the observed frequency in each bin. Step 3: plot the diagram, compute the Brier score, and judge whether the model is overconfident or underconfident. Step 4: recalibrate if needed, and report the score before and after. The demo runs a small worked example of the scoring step with illustrative design values: it takes five predicted probabilities, 0.85, 0.7, 0.5, 0.3, and 0.15, against outcomes of 1, 1, 0, 0, and 0, and reports the Brier score and the mean predicted probability. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"brier_design": 0.095, "mean_p_design": 0.5, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the studio diagrams, scores, and recalibrates, and the worked example shows the calibration arithmetic. The squared gaps are 0.0225, 0.09, 0.25, 0.09, and 0.0225, which sum to 0.475, so the Brier score is 0.095, and the mean predicted probability is 0.5, with 4 hits out of 5. The result that matters is the design judgment: a studio that shows the diagram and the score together is honest, and a score alone is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: brier: 0.095, hits: 4, mean_p: 0.5, n: 5.

When It Applies

The Signature Calibration Studio design applies as a study for anyone calibrating a probabilistic model: it shows how to diagram, score, and recalibrate. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the probabilities are not used, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The studio is a design, not a system, so it has never calibrated a real model, and its probabilities are illustrative. The design assumes the bins are large enough to be stable, which needs data, and a model calibrated on one population may not be calibrated on another. The design is a study of how calibration should be done, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a studio exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the Brier score. The five predictions and outcomes give squared gaps of 0.0225, 0.09, 0.25, 0.09, and 0.0225, which sum to 0.475, and 0.475 divided by 5 is 0.095. The mean predicted probability is 2.5 divided by 5, which is 0.5, and 4 of the 5 predictions were hits. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: Brier = (1/n) * sum (p_i - o_i)^2, where p_i is the predicted probability and o_i is 1 when the event happened and 0 when it did not. The equation states the calibration check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted probabilities and outcomes, squares each gap, and averages them for the Brier score.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of honest probabilities: a probability is a statement about frequency, and it can be checked by counting. The design follows the public evidence that calibration is a property of the model that can be fixed by recalibration, and it makes the fixing part of the studio. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A predicted probability is calibrated when events it calls likely actually happen often; the Brier score measures that gap, with 0 for perfect prediction.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
probs = [(0.85, 1), (0.7, 1), (0.5, 0), (0.3, 0), (0.15, 0)]
def compute():
    brier = round(sum((p - o) ** 2 for p, o in probs) / len(probs), 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in probs) / len(probs), 4)
    hits = sum(1 for p, o in probs if (p >= 0.5) == bool(o))
    n = len(probs)
    out = {"brier": brier, "mean_p": mean_p, "hits": hits, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Drift Monitor: A Design for Watching Models Decay JAH-PREDS-000013 · 13

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Drift Monitor is a Signature-made design for watching a predictive model decay, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design measures the model's error continuously, watches the input distribution for change, and raises the alarm when either moves beyond a threshold, so that the model is retrained before the damage is done. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of concept drift and production monitoring. The design takes the discipline the public record shows, that a model without monitoring will fail silently, and makes the monitor part of the model rather than an addition to it. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Drift Monitor is a Signature-made design for watching a predictive model decay, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design measures the model's error continuously, watches the input distribution for change, and raises the alarm when either moves beyond a threshold, so that the model is retrained before the damage is done. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Drift Monitor, a Signature-made design-study for detecting concept drift, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: measure the model's error continuously as predictions and outcomes arrive. Step 2: watch the input distribution, comparing recent inputs with the training inputs. Step 3: raise the alarm when the error rises or the distribution shifts beyond a threshold. Step 4: retrain on recent data, and report the before and after. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step with illustrative design values: it takes five monthly error rates, 0.11, 0.13, 0.17, 0.22, and 0.28, against a baseline rate of 0.11, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_design": 0.072, "rmse_design": 0.0949, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the monitor measures, watches, alarms, and retrains, and the worked example shows the drift arithmetic. The gaps from the baseline are 0.0, 0.02, 0.06, 0.11, and 0.17, giving a mean absolute error of 0.072 and a root mean square error of 0.0949, with a largest gap of 0.17. The result that matters is the design judgment: a monitor that alarms by the third month is useful, and one that alarms never is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.072, max_err: 0.17, n: 5, rmse: 0.0949.

When It Applies

The Signature Drift Monitor design applies as a study for anyone putting a model into production: it shows how to measure, watch, alarm, and retrain. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the model is never monitored, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The monitor is a design, not a system, so it has never watched a real model, and its rates are illustrative. The design assumes the drift can be detected before the damage is done, which is not always true, and a monitor that alarms too often will be ignored. The design is a study of how drift should be watched, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a monitor exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.0, 0.02, 0.06, 0.11, and 0.17, which sum to 0.36, so the mean absolute error is 0.36 divided by 5, which is 0.072. The squared gaps are 0.0, 0.0004, 0.0036, 0.0121, and 0.0289, which sum to 0.045, so the mean squared error is 0.009 and the root mean square error is the square root of 0.009, which is 0.0949. The largest single gap is 0.17. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of non-stationarity: the relationship between inputs and outcomes changes over time, and a model fitted to the past will fail on the future to the extent that it has changed. The design follows the public evidence that drift is sudden, gradual, or recurring, and it watches both the error and the inputs, because either can signal the change first. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.11, 0.11), (0.13, 0.11), (0.17, 0.11), (0.22, 0.11), (0.28, 0.11)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
