import hashlib
class ProgJAHPREDS000013:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(0.11, 0.11), (0.13, 0.11), (0.17, 0.11), (0.22, 0.11), (0.28, 0.11)]
        def compute():
            errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
            mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
            rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
            max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
            n = len(pairs)
            out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Feature Studio: A Design for Building Good Inputs JAH-PREDS-000014 · 14

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Feature Studio is a Signature-made design for building the inputs a model needs, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design gives the modeler a bench for building lags, ratios, rolling means, and calendar effects, and for testing each one, keeping the features that help and dropping the ones that do not. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of feature engineering as the craft of prediction. The design takes the lesson the public record shows, that a simple model on good features beats a complex model on raw inputs, and makes the testing part of the bench, so that no feature is kept without evidence. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Feature Studio is a Signature-made design for building the inputs a model needs, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design gives the modeler a bench for building lags, ratios, rolling means, and calendar effects, and for testing each one, keeping the features that help and dropping the ones that do not. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Feature Studio, a Signature-made design-study for feature engineering, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: list the quantities that should matter, from the domain knowledge. Step 2: build the features, lags, differences, ratios, rolling means, and calendar effects. Step 3: fit the model on the features, and check which ones matter. Step 4: keep the features that help, drop the ones that do not, and document the rest. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step with illustrative design values: it takes five feature groups with features built of 10, 7, 13, 5, and 9, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"total_features_design": 44, "mean_features_design": 8.8, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the studio builds, tests, keeps, and documents, and the worked example shows the counting arithmetic. The five groups build 10, 7, 13, 5, and 9 features, for a total of 44, a mean of 8.8, a smallest of 5, and a largest of 13. The result that matters is the design judgment: a bench that tests every feature is honest, and one that keeps features without evidence is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 13, mean: 8.8, n: 5, smallest: 5, total: 44.

When It Applies

The Signature Feature Studio design applies as a study for anyone building a model from data: it shows how to build, test, keep, and document features. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the domain is not understood, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The studio is a design, not a system, so it has never built a real feature, and its counts are illustrative. The design assumes the domain is understood well enough to list what should matter, which is not always true, and a feature that encodes a false belief will mislead the model faithfully. The design is a study of how features should be built and tested, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a studio exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five groups build 10, 7, 13, 5, and 9 features, which sum to 44, and 44 divided by 5 is 8.8. The smallest group builds 5 features and the largest builds 13. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with, and a design that cannot be checked is only a picture. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of representation: a model can only learn what its inputs express, so the inputs must express the structure of the problem. The design follows the public evidence that the best features come from understanding the domain, and it makes the documentation part of the bench, so that every feature carries its reason. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('lags', 10), ('ratios', 7), ('rolling', 13), ('calendar', 5), ('domain', 9)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPREDS000014:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('lags', 10), ('ratios', 7), ('rolling', 13), ('calendar', 5), ('domain', 9)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":

Signature Ensemble Builder: A Design for Committees of Models JAH-PREDS-000015 · 15

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Ensemble Builder is a Signature-made design for combining many models into one prediction, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design fits many simple models on resampled data, averages their answers, and reports the gain over a single model, with the diversity of the members measured alongside the gain. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of ensemble methods from random forests and gradient boosting, both published in 2001. The design takes the committee idea, that averaging reduces variance and sequential correction reduces bias, and adds the diversity check: the builder measures how much the members disagree, and refuses to average members that agree too much, because averaging identical models gains nothing. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Ensemble Builder is a Signature-made design for combining many models into one prediction, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design fits many simple models on resampled data, averages their answers, and reports the gain over a single model, with the diversity of the members measured alongside the gain. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Ensemble Builder, a Signature-made design-study for combining many models, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: resample the data to make many training sets. Step 2: fit a simple model on each set. Step 3: measure the diversity of the members, and combine them by averaging or voting. Step 4: report the ensemble's error against the single model's error, with the diversity alongside. The demo runs a small worked example of the comparison step with illustrative design values: it takes five ensemble errors of 1.6, 1.9, 1.4, 1.8, and 1.7 against single-model errors of 2.4, 2.7, 2.2, 2.6, and 2.5, and reports the mean absolute gap and the root mean square gap. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_gap_design": 0.8, "rmse_gap_design": 0.8, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the builder resamples, fits, measures diversity, and combines, and the worked example shows the comparison arithmetic. The gaps are 0.8, 0.8, 0.8, 0.8, and 0.8, giving a mean absolute gap of 0.8 and a root mean square gap of 0.8, with a largest gap of 0.8. The result that matters is the design judgment: a committee whose members disagree is wiser than any single member, and one whose members agree is only a single model in disguise, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.8, max_err: 0.8, n: 5, rmse: 0.8.

When It Applies

The Signature Ensemble Builder design applies as a study for anyone combining models: it shows how to resample, fit, measure diversity, and combine. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where interpretability is required, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The builder is a design, not a system, so it has never combined a real committee, and its errors are illustrative. The design assumes the members can be made diverse, which is not always true, and an ensemble is harder to interpret than a single model. The design is a study of how an ensemble should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a builder exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.8, 0.8, 0.8, 0.8, and 0.8, which sum to 4.0, so the mean absolute gap is 4.0 divided by 5, which is 0.8. The squared gaps are 0.64, 0.64, 0.64, 0.64, and 0.64, which sum to 3.2, so the mean squared gap is 0.64 and the root mean square gap is the square root of 0.64, which is 0.8. The largest single gap is 0.8. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of variance and bias: a single model has high variance, and averaging many cancels the variance while keeping the signal, while sequential correction reduces the bias. The design follows the public evidence that ensembles win competitions, and it adds the diversity check, because the gain comes from diversity rather than from any single method. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(1.6, 2.4), (1.9, 2.7), (1.4, 2.2), (1.8, 2.6), (1.7, 2.5)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

class ProgJAHPREDS000015:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(1.6, 2.4), (1.9, 2.7), (1.4, 2.2), (1.8, 2.6), (1.7, 2.5)]
        def compute():
            errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
            mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
            rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
            max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
            n = len(pairs)
            out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()

        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Neural Forecaster: A Design for Learning Long Dependencies JAH-PREDS-000016 · 16

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Neural Forecaster is a Signature-made design for forecasting a series with a network that learns long dependencies, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design windows the series into input-output pairs, trains a memory or attention network on them, and compares the result with the classical baseline, reporting the gain, the data required, and the conditions under which the gain holds. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of LSTM networks from 1997 and transformers from 2017. The design takes the learned-representation idea, that a network can discover dependencies without being told what they are, and adds the baseline rule: the network is only kept when it beats the classical method on held-out data, and the gain is reported with the data it cost. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Neural Forecaster is a Signature-made design for forecasting a series with a network that learns long dependencies, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design windows the series into input-output pairs, trains a memory or attention network on them, and compares the result with the classical baseline, reporting the gain, the data required, and the conditions under which the gain holds. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Neural Forecaster, a Signature-made design-study for neural time-series forecasting, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: window the series into input-output pairs. Step 2: train the network on the windows, with a validation set held out. Step 3: measure the error on the held-out windows, and compare it with the classical baseline. Step 4: report the gain, the data required, and the conditions under which the gain holds. The demo runs a small worked example of the comparison step with illustrative design values: it takes five neural forecast errors of 2.0, 1.8, 2.3, 1.9, and 2.1 against classical baseline errors of 2.6, 2.5, 2.8, 2.6, and 2.7, and reports the mean absolute gap and the root mean square gap. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_gap_design": 0.62, "rmse_gap_design": 0.6245, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the forecaster windows, trains, compares, and reports the gain with its cost, and the worked example shows the comparison arithmetic. The gaps are 0.6, 0.7, 0.5, 0.7, and 0.6, giving a mean absolute gap of 0.62 and a root mean square gap of 0.6245, with a largest gap of 0.7. The result that matters is the design judgment: a network that beats the baseline on held-out data is worth its cost, and one that does not is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.62, max_err: 0.7, n: 5, rmse: 0.6245.

When It Applies

The Signature Neural Forecaster design applies as a study for anyone building a neural forecaster: it shows how to window, train, compare, and report the gain with its cost. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the data is thin, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The forecaster is a design, not a system, so it has never forecast a real series, and its errors are illustrative. The design assumes the data is plentiful, which real series often are not, and it is opaque, so its forecasts cannot be explained. The design is a study of how a neural forecaster should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a forecaster exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.6, 0.7, 0.5, 0.7, and 0.6, which sum to 3.1, so the mean absolute gap is 3.1 divided by 5, which is 0.62. The squared gaps are 0.36, 0.49, 0.25, 0.49, and 0.36, which sum to 1.95, so the mean squared gap is 0.39 and the root mean square gap is the square root of 0.39, which is 0.6245. The largest single gap is 0.7. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of learned representation: a network with memory or attention can discover long-range dependencies that a classical model would miss. The design follows the public evidence that neural forecasting wins where the data is plentiful and loses where it is thin, and it makes the baseline comparison the gate. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. import hashlib
pairs = [(2.0, 2.6), (1.8, 2.5), (2.3, 2.8), (1.9, 2.6), (2.1, 2.7)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(5, 6), (8, 7), (3, 4), (10, 9), (6, 7)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPREDS000017:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(5, 6), (8, 7), (3, 4), (10, 9), (6, 7)]
        def compute():
            errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
            mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
            rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
            max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
            n = len(pairs)
            out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}

Signature Survival Engine: A Design for Predicting Time Until an Event JAH-PREDS-000018 · 18

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Survival Engine is a Signature-made design for predicting how long until an event, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes each subject's history, estimates the hazard, and predicts the survival curve, reporting the curve rather than a single time, with the censoring stated and the assumptions checked. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of survival analysis from Kaplan-Meier in 1958 and the Cox model in 1972. The design takes the curve idea, that a survival prediction is a curve rather than a number, and makes it a requirement: the engine never reports a single predicted time without the curve behind it. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Survival Engine is a Signature-made design for predicting how long until an event, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes each subject's history, estimates the hazard, and predicts the survival curve, reporting the curve rather than a single time, with the censoring stated and the assumptions checked. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Survival Engine, a Signature-made design-study for time-to-event prediction, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take each subject's history as features and an observed time, with a flag for censoring. Step 2: estimate the survival curve, and fit the model for the effects of the features. Step 3: predict each new subject's survival curve from the model. Step 4: report the curve, the concordance, and the censoring, and check the assumptions. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step with illustrative design values: it takes five predicted survival times, 20, 32, 16, 44, and 28 months, against observed times of 18, 34, 18, 42, and 30 months, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_design": 2.0, "rmse_design": 2.0, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the engine estimates the curve, reports it, and checks the assumptions, and the worked example shows the error arithmetic. The gaps are 2, 2, 2, 2, and 2, giving a mean absolute error of 2.0 and a root mean square error of 2.0, with a largest gap of 2. The result that matters is the design judgment: a survival prediction reported as a curve is honest, and one reported as a single time is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 2.0, max_err: 2, n: 5, rmse: 2.0.

When It Applies

The Signature Survival Engine design applies as a study for anyone building a time-to-event predictor: it shows how to estimate the curve, report it, and check the assumptions. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the hazards are not proportional, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The engine is a design, not a system, so it has never predicted a real survival, and its values are illustrative. The design assumes the hazards are proportional and the censoring is uninformative, which may not hold, and a single predicted time loses the uncertainty the curve carries. The design is a study of how a survival engine should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such an engine exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 2, 2, 2, 2, and 2, which sum to 10, so the mean absolute error is 10 divided by 5, which is 2.0. The squared gaps are 4, 4, 4, 4, and 4, which sum to 20, so the mean squared error is 4.0 and the root mean square error is the square root of 4.0, which is 2.0. The largest single gap is 2. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of time-to-event with censoring: some subjects are still event-free when the study ends, and their information is that they survived at least that long, which must be used rather than discarded. The design follows the public evidence that the Cox model and Kaplan-Meier handle censoring correctly, and it makes the censoring part of the report. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. import hashlib
pairs = [(20, 18), (32, 34), (16, 18), (44, 42), (28, 30)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Anomaly Engine: A Design for Flagging the Exception JAH-PREDS-000019 · 19

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Anomaly Engine is a Signature-made design for flagging the case that does not fit, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design learns the normal pattern, scores each new case by how far it lies from normal, and applies a threshold set with the users, reporting the catches, the false alarms, and the misses. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of anomaly detection from statistical control through fraud detection in the 1980s and 1990s. The design takes the threshold problem, that where the boundary is drawn decides the trade between false alarms and missed events, and makes the threshold a joint decision with the users rather than a number chosen by the builder. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Anomaly Engine is a Signature-made design for flagging the case that does not fit, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design learns the normal pattern, scores each new case by how far it lies from normal, and applies a threshold set with the users, reporting the catches, the false alarms, and the misses. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Anomaly Engine, a Signature-made design-study for detecting rare events, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: learn the normal pattern from the normal data. Step 2: score each new case by its distance from normal. Step 3: apply the threshold, set with the users, and flag the cases above it. Step 4: report the catches, the false alarms, and the misses, and adjust the threshold. The demo runs a small worked example of the scoring step with illustrative design values: it takes five anomaly scores, 0.9, 0.35, 0.85, 0.25, and 0.7, against outcomes of 1, 0, 1, 0, and 1, and reports the Brier score and the mean predicted probability. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"brier_design": 0.0615, "mean_p_design": 0.61, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the engine learns normal, scores, thresholds with the users, and reports all three outcomes, and the worked example shows the calibration arithmetic. The squared gaps are 0.01, 0.1225, 0.0225, 0.0625, and 0.09, which sum to 0.3075, so the Brier score is 0.0615, and the mean predicted probability is 0.61, with 5 hits out of 5. The result that matters is the design judgment: a detector that reports its false alarms is honest, and one that reports only its catches is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: brier: 0.0615, hits: 5, mean_p: 0.61, n: 5.

When It Applies

The Signature Anomaly Engine design applies as a study for anyone building a detector: it shows how to learn normal, score, threshold with the users, and report all three outcomes. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where normal is not stable, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The engine is a design, not a system, so it has never flagged a real anomaly, and its scores are illustrative. The design assumes normal is stable enough to learn, which is not always true, and an adversary can learn the boundary and stay inside it. The design is a study of how an anomaly engine should be built, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such an engine exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the Brier score. The five predictions and outcomes give squared gaps of 0.01, 0.1225, 0.0225, 0.0625, and 0.09, which sum to 0.3075, and 0.3075 divided by 5 is 0.0615. The mean predicted probability is 3.05 divided by 5, which is 0.61, and 5 of the 5 predictions were hits. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: Brier = (1/n) * sum (p_i - o_i)^2, where p_i is the predicted probability and o_i is 1 when the event happened and 0 when it did not. The equation states the calibration check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted probabilities and outcomes, squares each gap, and averages them for the Brier score.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of the normal: a model of normal behaviour is a model of what to expect, and an anomaly is a case the model did not expect. The design follows the public evidence that normal drifts and a model that does not adapt will flag the new normal as anomalous, and it adds the adaptation: the normal pattern is re-learned on a schedule. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A predicted probability is calibrated when events it calls likely actually happen often; the Brier score measures that gap, with 0 for perfect prediction.. History

The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Problem

Method

Step 1: learn the normal pattern from the normal data. Step 2: score each new case by its distance from normal. Step 3: apply the threshold, set with the users, and flag the cases above it. Step 4: report the catches, the false alarms, and the misses, and adjust the threshold. The demo runs a small worked example of the scoring step with illustrative design values: it takes five anomaly scores, 0.9, 0.35, 0.85, 0.25, and 0.7, against outcomes of 1, 0, 1, 0, and 1, and reports the Brier score and the mean predicted probability. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements.

Working demo program

import hashlib
probs = [(0.9, 1), (0.35, 0), (0.85, 1), (0.25, 0), (0.7, 1)]
def compute():
    brier = round(sum((p - o) ** 2 for p, o in probs) / len(probs), 4)
    mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in probs) / len(probs), 4)
    hits = sum(1 for p, o in probs if (p >= 0.5) == bool(o))
    n = len(probs)
    out = {"brier": brier, "mean_p": mean_p, "hits": hits, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPREDS000019:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        probs = [(0.9, 1), (0.35, 0), (0.85, 1), (0.25, 0), (0.7, 1)]
        def compute():
            brier = round(sum((p - o) ** 2 for p, o in probs) / len(probs), 4)
            mean_p = round(sum(p for p, _ in probs) / len(probs), 4)
            hits = sum(1 for p, o in probs if (p >= 0.5) == bool(o))
            n = len(probs)
            out = {"brier": brier, "mean_p": mean_p, "hits": hits, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPREDS000019().run())

Signature Fairness Auditor: A Design for Measuring Uneven Errors JAH-PREDS-000020 · 20

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Fairness Auditor is a Signature-made design for auditing a predictive model for bias, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the model's predictions and outcomes, splits them by group, computes the error rates for each group, and reports the differences with the calibration, so that the trade-off between fairness definitions is visible. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of bias in predictive models and the ProPublica analysis of COMPAS in 2016. The design takes the lesson of that debate, that calibration and equal error rates cannot both hold when the base rates differ, and makes the trade-off part of the report: the auditor shows several fairness metrics at once, and states which one was chosen and why. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Fairness Auditor is a Signature-made design for auditing a predictive model for bias, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes the model's predictions and outcomes, splits them by group, computes the error rates for each group, and reports the differences with the calibration, so that the trade-off between fairness definitions is visible. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Fairness Auditor, a Signature-made design-study for auditing predictive models for bias, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the model's predictions and outcomes, split by group. Step 2: compute the error rates for each group, the false positives and the false negatives. Step 3: compute the calibration for each group, and compare the rates across groups. Step 4: report the rates, the calibration, and the trade-off, and state which fairness definition was chosen. The demo runs a small worked example of the comparison step with illustrative design values: it takes five group error rates, 0.2, 0.17, 0.23, 0.19, and 0.21, against a reference rate of 0.2, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_design": 0.016, "rmse_design": 0.02, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the auditor computes error rates by group, checks calibration, and states the trade-off, and the worked example shows the comparison arithmetic. The gaps are 0.0, 0.03, 0.03, 0.01, and 0.01, giving a mean absolute error of 0.016 and a root mean square error of 0.02, with a largest gap of 0.03. The result that matters is the design judgment: an auditor that shows several metrics and states its choice can be debated, and one that shows a single metric cannot, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 0.016, max_err: 0.03, n: 5, rmse: 0.02.

When It Applies

The Signature Fairness Auditor design applies as a study for anyone auditing a predictive model: it shows how to compute error rates by group, check calibration, and state the trade-off. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply as a single metric, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The auditor is a design, not a system, so it has never audited a real model, and its rates are illustrative. The design assumes the groups are defined and the data is available, which is not always true, and it cannot choose between fairness definitions, because the choice is a value judgment. The design is a study of how an audit should be done, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such an auditor exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 0.0, 0.03, 0.03, 0.01, and 0.01, which sum to 0.08, so the mean absolute error is 0.08 divided by 5, which is 0.016. The squared gaps are 0.0, 0.0009, 0.0009, 0.0001, and 0.0001, which sum to 0.002, so the mean squared error is 0.0004 and the root mean square error is the square root of 0.0004, which is 0.02. The largest single gap is 0.03. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of error distribution: a model's errors are not spread evenly, and the unevenness follows the inequalities in the data. The design follows the public evidence that the choice between fairness definitions is a policy choice rather than a technical one, and it makes the choice explicit in the report. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(0.2, 0.2), (0.17, 0.2), (0.23, 0.2), (0.19, 0.2), (0.21, 0.2)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Signature Interval Forecaster: A Design for Ranges with Coverage JAH-PREDS-000021 · 21

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Interval Forecaster is a Signature-made design for forecasting a range rather than a point, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design produces a distribution, reads the prediction interval from it, and checks the coverage, recalibrating when the interval covers the truth more or less often than it claims. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of prediction intervals in weather, energy, and finance. The design takes the coverage discipline the public record shows, that an interval claiming ninety percent coverage must cover the truth about ninety percent of the time, and makes the coverage check part of the design rather than an afterthought. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Interval Forecaster is a Signature-made design for forecasting a range rather than a point, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design produces a distribution, reads the prediction interval from it, and checks the coverage, recalibrating when the interval covers the truth more or less often than it claims. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Interval Forecaster, a Signature-made design-study for prediction intervals, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: produce a distribution for the forecast, by quantile regression or by an ensemble. Step 2: read the prediction interval from the distribution. Step 3: when the actuals arrive, check how often they fall inside the interval. Step 4: recalibrate if the coverage differs from the stated probability, and report the coverage. The demo runs a small worked example of the measurement step with illustrative design values: it takes five interval widths, 11, 14, 9, 17, and 13 units, against a target width of 12, and reports the mean absolute error and the root mean square error. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"mae_design": 2.4, "rmse_design": 2.8284, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the forecaster produces a distribution, reads the interval, and checks the coverage, and the worked example shows the comparison arithmetic. The gaps are 1, 2, 3, 5, and 1, giving a mean absolute error of 2.4 and a root mean square error of 2.8284, with a largest gap of 5. The result that matters is the design judgment: an interval that is checked for coverage is honest, and one that is never checked is not, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: mae: 2.4, max_err: 5, n: 5, rmse: 2.8284.

When It Applies

The Signature Interval Forecaster design applies as a study for anyone forecasting a range: it shows how to produce a distribution, read the interval, and check the coverage. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where a single number is presented as certain, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The forecaster is a design, not a system, so it has never forecast a real range, and its values are illustrative. The design assumes the distribution can be estimated, which is not always true, and an interval can be calibrated on average and wrong in the cases that matter. The design is a study of how a range should be forecast, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a forecaster exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of error measurement. The five gaps are 1, 2, 3, 5, and 1, which sum to 12, so the mean absolute error is 12 divided by 5, which is 2.4. The squared gaps are 1, 4, 9, 25, and 1, which sum to 40, so the mean squared error is 8.0 and the root mean square error is the square root of 8.0, which is 2.8284. The largest single gap is 5. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with. Formal statement: MAE = (1/n) * sum |pred_i - actual_i|; RMSE = sqrt((1/n) * sum (pred_i - actual_i)^2); max error = max |pred_i - actual_i|. The equation states the forecast-error check the demo performs: it takes the worked example's predicted and actual pairs, averages the absolute errors for the MAE, and takes the square root of the averaged squared errors for the RMSE.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of uncertainty as a quantity: the future is not a number but a distribution, and the forecast's job is to describe that distribution honestly. The design follows the public evidence that a point forecast without its interval is a promise without its risk, and it makes the interval the forecast's headline. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A forecast is judged by the gap between what was predicted and what happened; the MAE averages that gap and the RMSE punishes large gaps more heavily.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Working demo program

import hashlib
pairs = [(11, 12), (14, 12), (9, 12), (17, 12), (13, 12)]
def compute():
    errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
    mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
    rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
    max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
    n = len(pairs)
    out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPREDS000021:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        pairs = [(11, 12), (14, 12), (9, 12), (17, 12), (13, 12)]
        def compute():
            errs = [abs(p - a) for p, a in pairs]
            mae = round(sum(errs) / len(errs), 4)
            rmse = round((sum(e * e for e in errs) / len(errs)) ** 0.5, 4)
            max_err = round(max(errs), 4)
            n = len(pairs)
            out = {"mae": mae, "rmse": rmse, "max_err": max_err, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPREDS000021().run())

Signature Forecast Explainer: A Design for Showing Why a Forecast Says What It Says JAH-PREDS-000022 · 22

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Forecast Explainer is a Signature-made design for showing why a forecast says what it says, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes a forecast, decomposes it into its components, level, trend, season, and the contribution of each feature, and reports the decomposition alongside the forecast, so that the user can see what drove it. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of explanation in expert systems and in modern explainable AI. The design takes the lesson of that history, that a decision without a reason cannot be checked, and applies it to forecasting: a forecast without its decomposition cannot be questioned. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Forecast Explainer is a Signature-made design for showing why a forecast says what it says, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes a forecast, decomposes it into its components, level, trend, season, and the contribution of each feature, and reports the decomposition alongside the forecast, so that the user can see what drove it. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Forecast Explainer, a Signature-made design-study for explaining forecasts, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the forecast and its model. Step 2: decompose the forecast into its components, and compute each component's contribution. Step 3: rank the contributions, and report the largest ones with their values. Step 4: present the forecast with its decomposition, so the user can question it. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step with illustrative design values: it takes five forecast components with contributions of 40, 25, 55, 30, and 45 units, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"total_contribution_design": 195, "mean_contribution_design": 39.0, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the explainer decomposes, ranks, and reports, and the worked example shows the counting arithmetic. The five contributions 40, 25, 55, 30, and 45 total 195, with a mean of 39.0, a smallest of 25, and a largest of 55. The result that matters is the design judgment: a forecast that shows its components can be questioned, and one that does not cannot, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 55, mean: 39.0, n: 5, smallest: 25, total: 195.

When It Applies

The Signature Forecast Explainer design applies as a study for anyone building a forecaster that must be questioned: it shows how to decompose, rank, and report. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the model cannot be decomposed, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The explainer is a design, not a system, so it has never explained a real forecast, and its contributions are illustrative. The design assumes the forecast can be decomposed, which is not true of every model, and a decomposition can be misleading when the components interact. The design is a study of how a forecast should be explained, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such an explainer exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five contributions 40, 25, 55, 30, and 45 add to 195, and 195 divided by 5 is 39.0. The smallest contribution is 25 and the largest is 55. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with, and a design that cannot be checked is only a picture. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of trust: a user accepts a forecast when the user can see what drove it, check the parts, and find the mistake when there is one. The design follows the public evidence that explanation facilities were the reason expert systems were trusted, and it applies the same requirement to forecasting. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('level', 40), ('trend', 25), ('season', 55), ('feature 1', 30), ('feature 2', 45)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPREDS000022:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('level', 40), ('trend', 25), ('season', 55), ('feature 1', 30), ('feature 2', 45)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPREDS000022().run())

Signature Failure-Mode Lab: A Design for Stress-Testing Forecasts JAH-PREDS-000023 · 23

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Failure-Mode Lab is a Signature-made design for stress-testing a predictive model, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes a model, runs it on scenarios outside its history, including breaks, regime changes, and black swans, and reports where it breaks and by how much. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of the limits of prediction, from Goodhart's law in 1975 through the 2008 crisis. The design takes the lesson of that history, that a model works in normal times and fails in the times that matter, and makes the stress test part of the design: no model is reported without its stress errors. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Failure-Mode Lab is a Signature-made design for stress-testing a predictive model, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design takes a model, runs it on scenarios outside its history, including breaks, regime changes, and black swans, and reports where it breaks and by how much. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Failure-Mode Lab, a Signature-made design-study for stress-testing predictive models, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: take the model and its training history. Step 2: build stress scenarios outside the history, breaks, regime changes, and rare events. Step 3: run the model on the scenarios, and measure the error in each. Step 4: report the stress errors alongside the normal ones, and name the scenarios where the model breaks. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step with illustrative design values: it takes five stress scenarios with model errors of 7, 11, 5, 14, and 8 units, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"total_error_design": 45, "mean_error_design": 9.0, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the lab builds scenarios, runs them, and reports the stress errors, and the worked example shows the counting arithmetic. The five scenarios give errors of 7, 11, 5, 14, and 8, for a total of 45, a mean of 9.0, a smallest of 5, and a largest of 14. The result that matters is the design judgment: a model whose stress errors are measured can be used with its limits known, and one whose are not cannot, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 14, mean: 9.0, n: 5, smallest: 5, total: 45.

When It Applies

The Signature Failure-Mode Lab design applies as a study for anyone testing a predictive model: it shows how to build scenarios, run them, and report the stress errors. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the scenarios cannot be imagined, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The lab is a design, not a system, so it has never stress-tested a real model, and its errors are illustrative. The design assumes the stress scenarios can be imagined, which is the hard part, because a black swan is by definition unimagined. The design is a study of how a model should be stress-tested, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a lab exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five scenarios give errors of 7, 11, 5, 14, and 8, which sum to 45, and 45 divided by 5 is 9.0. The smallest error is 5 and the largest is 14. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with, and a design that cannot be checked is only a picture. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of induction: a model learns from the past, and the past does not contain the future's novelties. The design follows the public evidence that the 2008 crisis defeated models fitted to a history without a nationwide housing crash, and it answers with the stress test, which asks what the model does outside its history. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. Scientific relation: A ledger of counts is summarised by its total, its mean, and its extremes; those four numbers are the checkable summary of the record's figures.. The demo recomputes the checkable arithmetic of this record from the stated figures, runs the computation twice, and compares the two SHA-256 digests; matching digests show the arithmetic is reproducible, and the method text states every input figure.

Demo

The demo runs the computation described in the method and prints the named results twice, with a SHA-256 digest of each run: Re-run the demo code embedded in this record: it prints the named results twice with a SHA-256 digest of each run and reports Deterministic: MATCH when the two runs agree. Cross-check the historical facts against the cited sources: Forecasting, Overfitting, Concept drift. Last verified 2026-11-16.

Working demo program

import hashlib
ledger = [('scenario 1', 7), ('scenario 2', 11), ('scenario 3', 5), ('scenario 4', 14), ('scenario 5', 8)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPREDS000023:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('scenario 1', 7), ('scenario 2', 11), ('scenario 3', 5), ('scenario 4', 14), ('scenario 5', 8)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPREDS000023().run())
