Signature Forecast Workbench: A Design for the Whole Forecasting Craft JAH-PREDS-000024 · 24

Evidence: design-study Signature-made design-study: all design values are illustrative design values, never presented as real historical fact or as an established result.

The Signature Forecast Workbench is a Signature-made design for the whole craft of forecasting, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design gathers the parts the other designs in this edition describe separately, the engine, the feature studio, the calibration studio, the drift monitor, and the failure-mode lab, into one workbench, so that a forecaster can move from data to a checked, calibrated, monitored forecast without changing tools. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

History

The design-study is dated 2026 as a Signature design, and it builds on the public history of forecasting from ARIMA in 1970 through the machine-learning methods of the 2010s. The design takes the lesson of that history, that forecasting is a cycle from data through fitting, checking, and monitoring, and makes the cycle the architecture: one workbench, five stations, and a ledger that follows every forecast from the data it was fitted on to the audit where it is checked. The history in this record is the public history it builds on, and the design itself is new, Signature-made, and labeled as a study throughout.

Description

The Signature Forecast Workbench is a Signature-made design for the whole craft of forecasting, drawn as a design-study rather than a description of any real product. The design gathers the parts the other designs in this edition describe separately, the engine, the feature studio, the calibration studio, the drift monitor, and the failure-mode lab, into one workbench, so that a forecaster can move from data to a checked, calibrated, monitored forecast without changing tools. The design is presented as a study, with illustrative values throughout, and it is never presented as a real system or as an established result.

The Problem

What is the Signature Forecast Workbench, a Signature-made design-study for the whole craft of forecasting, how does it work in practice, and what do its checkable figures show? This record answers that question with the dated history, the method, a worked demo whose figures are printed twice and hash-checked, and a plain statement of its limits.

The Worked Solution

Step 1: build the features in the studio. Step 2: fit the model in the engine, and check the residuals. Step 3: calibrate the probabilities in the studio, and explain the forecast. Step 4: monitor the forecast in production, and stress-test it in the lab, on a schedule. The demo runs a small worked example of the counting step with illustrative design values: it takes five workbench stations with forecasts handled of 110, 85, 130, 100, and 120, and reports the total, the mean, the smallest, and the largest. The demo is a worked example of the design's arithmetic, and its values are illustrative design values, not measurements. The demo recomputes the checkable part of this solution from the key figures {"total_forecasts_design": 545, "mean_forecasts_design": 109.0, "design_study": true}.

The Answer

The design's checkable claims are its own arithmetic and its completeness as a design: the workbench covers features, fitting, calibration, monitoring, and stress-testing, and the worked example shows the counting arithmetic. The five stations handle 110, 85, 130, 100, and 120 forecasts, for a total of 545, a mean of 109.0, a smallest of 85, and a largest of 130. The result that matters is the design judgment: a craft needs one workbench for the whole cycle, because a cycle split across tools loses the ledger that ties a forecast to its data and its audit, even though the design itself is a study and not a product. The demo prints these checked figures: largest: 130, mean: 109.0, n: 5, smallest: 85, total: 545.

When It Applies

The Signature Forecast Workbench design applies as a study for anyone tooling the craft: it shows how to gather the stations into one workbench, and gives the arithmetic for checking the work. It applies as a design reference, not as a product, and only where the reader understands that the values are illustrative. It does not apply as evidence about any real system, it does not apply where the cycle is not the problem, and it does not apply where the design's assumptions have not been examined.

Limits

The design-study's limits are stated plainly. The workbench is a design, not a system, so it has never produced a real forecast, and its counts are illustrative. The design assumes the whole cycle can be served by one tool, which is a bet, and it cannot solve the drift, the black swan, or the gaming, it can only make them visible. The design is a study of how the craft should be tooled, and it must not be mistaken for evidence that such a workbench exists or works.

Math Proof

The mathematics is the mathematics of a ledger. The five stations handle 110, 85, 130, 100, and 120 forecasts, which sum to 545, and 545 divided by 5 is 109.0. The smallest station handles 85 forecasts and the largest handles 130. These are illustrative design values, and their transparency is the point: a design whose arithmetic can be checked is a design that can be argued with, and a design that cannot be checked is only a picture. Formal statement: total = sum of the counts; mean = total / n; smallest = min of the counts; largest = max of the counts. The equation states the ledger check the demo performs: it adds the named counts, divides by how many there are for the mean, and picks out the smallest and the largest.

Science Proof

The design-study's science is the science of the whole cycle: a forecast is not a number but a process, from data through fitting, checking, and monitoring, and a tool that covers only part of the process leaves the rest to chance. The design follows the public evidence that the forecasts which survived were the ones whose whole cycle was cared for, and it makes the cycle the architecture. The design is a study, so its claims are design claims, and they are judged by whether the design is complete and coherent, not by whether a real system was measured. import hashlib
ledger = [('features', 110), ('fitting', 85), ('calibration', 130), ('monitoring', 100), ('stress', 120)]
def compute():
    counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
    total = round(sum(counts), 4)
    mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
    smallest = min(counts)
    largest = max(counts)
    n = len(counts)
    out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
    return out
outputs = compute()
result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
outputs2 = compute()
result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
    print(f"{k}: {v}")
print(f"Result: {result}")
print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")

Full working program

import hashlib
class ProgJAHPREDS000024:
    """Working program for this record; run() recomputes the demo result."""
    def run(self):
        import hashlib
        ledger = [('features', 110), ('fitting', 85), ('calibration', 130), ('monitoring', 100), ('stress', 120)]
        def compute():
            counts = [c for _, c in ledger]
            total = round(sum(counts), 4)
            mean = round(sum(counts) / len(counts), 4)
            smallest = min(counts)
            largest = max(counts)
            n = len(counts)
            out = {"total": total, "mean": mean, "smallest": smallest, "largest": largest, "n": n}
            return out
        outputs = compute()
        result = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()))
        h1 = hashlib.sha256(result.encode()).hexdigest()
        outputs2 = compute()
        result2 = "|".join(f"{k}={v}" for k, v in sorted(outputs2.items()))
        h2 = hashlib.sha256(result2.encode()).hexdigest()
        for k, v in sorted(outputs.items()):
            print(f"{k}: {v}")
        print(f"Result: {result}")
        print(f"Run 1 SHA-256: {h1}")
        print(f"Run 2 SHA-256: {h2}")
        print(f"Deterministic: {'MATCH' if h1 == h2 else 'MISMATCH'}")
        return {"deterministic": h1 == h2, "result": result}
if __name__ == "__main__":
    print(ProgJAHPREDS000024().run())
